Gelfand–Kirillov dimensions and annihilator varieties of highest weight modules of exceptional Lie algebras
Zhanqiang Bai, Fan Gao, Yutong Wang, Xun Xie

Abstract
. In this paper, we give an efficient algorithm to compute the Gelfand–Kirillov dimensions of simple highest weight modules of exceptional Lie algebras. By using the Sommers–Achar duality, we also determine the annihilator varieties of these highest weight modules.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 22E47; Secondary 17B10, 17B08

1.
Introduction

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex semisimple Lie algebra and
U(\mathfrak{g})
be its enveloping algebra. The Gelfand–Kirillov (GK) dimension is an important invariant to measure the size of an infinite-dimensional
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M

[
GK66
]
.
It plays significant roles in representation theory, see for example
[
Vog78
,
Jos78
,
Jos81
]
.

Annihilator variety is another invariant for infinite-dimensional
U(\mathfrak{g})
-modules.
For a simple
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M
, let
I(M)=\mathrm{Ann}(M)
be its annihilator ideal in
U(\mathfrak{g})
and
J(M)
be the corresponding graded ideal in
S(\mathfrak{g}):=\text{gr}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. The zero set of
J(M)
in the dual vector space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
of
\mathfrak{g}
is called
the annihilator variety
of
M
, and is denoted by
V(\mathrm{Ann}(M))
. From Joseph
[
Jos78
]
, we know that
\dim V(\mathrm{Ann}(M))=2\operatorname{GKdim}M
.
In fact, Borho–Brylinski
[
BB82
]
proved that the annihilator variety of a highest weight module with a fixed regular integral infinitesimal
character is the Zariski closure of a nilpotent orbit in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Joseph
[
Jos85
]
extended this result to highest weight modules with arbitrary infinitesimal characters.
In general, the study of Gelfand–Kirillov dimensions and annihilator varieties of irreducible highest weight modules is closely related with many research fields, such as representations of Weyl groups, Kazhdan–Lusztig cells, representations of Lie groups and
W
-algebras. See for example
[
BV82
,
BMSZ23a
,
BMSZ23b
,
BMSZ25
,
GS13
,
GSK21
,
GT22
,
McG96
,
Los15
,
LY23
]
.

However, in general, these two invariants are not easy to compute for irreducible highest weight modules.
Nishiyama–Ochiai–Taniguchi
[
NOT01
]
and Enright–Willenbring
[
EW04
]
independently gave characterizations of the GK dimensions for unitary highest weight Harish-Chandra modules appearing in the dual pair settings. Bai–Hunziker
[
BH15
]
found a uniform formula for the GK dimensions of unitary highest weight modules.
By using the theory of Lusztig’s
\mathbf{a}
-function
[
Lus77
,
Lus79
,
Lus82
,
Lus84
,
Lus85a
,
Lus85b
]
, Bai–Xie
[
BX19b
]
also realized a simple algorithm to compute the GK dimension of a highest weight module of
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
, which generalized Joseph’s work on GK dimensions of regular integral highest weight modules for type
A
in
[
Jos78
]
.
Then Bai–Xiao–Xie
[
BXX23
]
gave certain explicit formulas of GK dimensions for simple highest weight modules of all classical Lie algebras and highest weight Harish-Chandra modules of exceptional Lie algebras.
For the trivial infinitesimal
character case, the computation of the annihilator variety of a highest weight module has been done in type
A
by Joseph
[
Jos80b
]
, in types
B

and
C
by Garfinkle
[
Gar93
]
, and in type
D
by McGovern and Pietraho

[
MT23
]
.
Furthermore, building upon the Robinson–Schensted insertion algorithm utilized in
[
BX19b
,
BXX23
]
, Bai–Ma–Wang
[
BMW25
]
gave a simple description for the nilpotent orbit associated with the annihilator variety of a highest weight module of classical Lie algebras. However, describing these two invariants for a general highest weight module of exceptional Lie algebras remains notably more intricate and challenging. This is one motivation for the present paper, devoted to this problem. On the other hand, Bai–Xiao
[
BX19a
]
recently proved that a scalar generalized Verma module is reducible if and only if the GK dimension of its corresponding highest weight module is strictly less than the dimension of the nilradical of the corresponding parabolic subalgebra. This also gives us another motivation to study the GK dimensions of arbitrary highest weight modules of exceptional Lie algebras.

From
[
BX19b
]
and
[
BXX23
]
, we know that the computation of the GK dimension of a highest weight module
L(\lambda)
with infinitesimal character
\lambda
can be reduced to the computation of Lusztig’s
\mathbf{a}
-function of a certain Weyl group element
w_{\lambda}
. For classical types, we can use the Robinson–Schensted (RS) insertion algorithm to compute the value of
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
directly without determining
w_{\lambda}
explicitly. For an exceptional type Lie algebra, the computation of
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
is more complicated since there is no RS algorithm and it is not easy to find out the corresponding Weyl group element
w_{\lambda}
. In view of this, we first seek to give an efficient algorithm to compute the value of
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
, which will help us with computing the GK dimensions and annihilator varieties of highest weight modules of exceptional Lie algebras.

Given an integral highest weight module
L(\lambda)
of an exceptional Lie algebra, this is achieved in two steps. First, we use Lemma
3.2
to obtain the
w_{\lambda}
such that
w_{\lambda}
is minimal and
w_{\lambda}^{-1}\lambda
is antidominant. Second, we use the computer algebra package PyCox written by Geck
[
Gec12
]
to determine the
value of
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
, which in turn determines
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
for integral
\lambda
.

Our first result in this paper is to generalize the above and to give an efficient algorithm to compute the value of
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
for a nonintegral weight
\lambda
, which also allows us to obtain
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
. This we elaborate now.

Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\Phi
be the root system of
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
and let
W
be its Weyl group. For any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, the integral Weyl subgroup is denoted by
W_{[\lambda]}
, whose root system is denoted by
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
. Suppose
\Phi_{[\lambda]}=\Phi_{[\lambda]_{1}}\times\Phi_{[\lambda]_{2}}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{[\lambda]_{k}}\simeq\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{k}
is a direct product of irreducible root systems, and we denote such an isomorphism by
\phi
. Thus we also get an isomorphism between the Cartan subalgebras
\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}=\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]_{1}}}\times\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]_{2}}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]_{k}}}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{1}}\times\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{k}}
, and also between their complex duals, both still denoted by
\phi
.
From the isomorphism
\phi
, we can write

\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]_{i}}}}).

Denote
{\lambda}^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})
.
Then
{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}:={\lambda}^{\prime}|_{{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{i}}}}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]_{i}}}})
is an integral weight of type
\Phi_{i}
.

1.1.
Main result

With these notations we have the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

For a given nonintegral weight
\lambda
, suppose that
\lambda=w_{\lambda}\mu
, where
\mu
is antidominant and
w_{\lambda}
is the unique minimal length element in the integral Weyl subgroup
W_{[\lambda]}
.
Suppose
\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{k}
is a direct product of some irreducible root systems, and we denote such an isomorphism by
\phi
. Then we have

\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=\mathbf{a}(w_{{\lambda}^{\prime}})=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\mathbf{a}(w_{{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}}).

In particular, when
{\Phi_{i}}
is of classical type, we can determine the value of
\mathbf{a}(w_{{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}})
directly from
{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}
without finding out
{w_{{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}}}
, by using the algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
.

From
\dim V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))=2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
, we see that the nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
associated with
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))
can be determined by
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
if there is only one such nilpotent orbit with dimension equal to
2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
. However, if there are more than one nilpotent orbits with dimension equal to
2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
, we need to resort to the result of Joseph
[
Jos85
]
to determine
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
explicitly.

For this purpose, we use
\pi_{w}\in{\rm Irr}(W)
to denote the special representation of
W
corresponding to the two-sided cell containing
w
, as in
[
BMW25
]
. For a special representation
\pi_{w}
, let
\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w})
be the associated special nilpotent orbit via the Springer correspondence.

From the isomorphism
\phi
, we see that
\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}
is an integral weight of type
{\Phi_{i}}
. When
\Phi_{i}
is of classical type, we use the RS algorithm and H-algorithm given in
[
BMW25
]
to obtain a special nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}}})
. When
\Phi_{i}
is of exceptional type, we use Lemma
3.2
and PyCox to get the corresponding character
\chi_{i}\in{\rm Irr}(W(\Phi_{i}))
of
w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}}
and the corresponding special nilpotent orbit. In this way, we see that
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=\prod\pi_{w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}}}\times\prod\chi_{j}
will be the special representation of
W_{[\lambda]}
and we have
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G}\circ j_{W_{[\lambda]}}^{W}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}})
by Joseph
[
Jos85
]
. Granted with
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}
, the computation of
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be further reduced to the Sommers duality as given in
[
Som01
]
. This gives us the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

Keep notations as above.
Let
H^{\vee}\subseteq G^{\vee}
be a pseudo-Levi with root system
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
. Let
H
be its Langlands dual group, whose root system is then
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
.
Now
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}\in{\rm Irr}(W(H))
is a special representation of
W(H)
. Let
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}
be the corresponding special representation of
W(H^{\vee})
obtained via the canonical isomorphism
W(H)\simeq W(H^{\vee})
. Furthermore, let
L^{\vee}\subseteq H^{\vee}
be a Levi subgroup of
H^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished nilpotent orbit of
L^{\vee}
such that
d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}))=G\cdot\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
.
Then

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{\Phi^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
d_{\rm Som}^{\Phi^{\vee}}
is the duality given in
[
Som01
]
.

The content and details of Theorem
1.2
are explained in §
7
. We just note here that it essentially follows from applying Sommers’ result in
[
Som98
,
Som01
]
to Joseph’s formula above, in order to compute
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
from
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}
in a more direct way.

To summarize, Theorem
1.1
, Theorem
1.2
and the usage of PyCox (whenever necessary) constitute the main algorithmic steps in our determining
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
. Moreover, the same as for the classical case treated in
[
BMW25
]
, we have incorporated all these in a simple online program. The program takes
\lambda
as the input and returns
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
. It is available at

http://test.slashblade.top:5000/lie/GKdim

We remark also that it is an interesting problem to determine all highest weight modules whose annihilator varieties are equal to a given nilpotent orbit closure, see
[
Irv85
,
Jos98
,
Tam23
,
BMXX24
,
BMW25
,
BZ25
]
for example. When
L(\lambda)
is a simple highest weight module such that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal (see Definition
2.15
), we tabulate all the possibilities of
\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}})
or
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}
in the Appendix, when
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
is equal to a fixed given nilpotent orbit.

This paper is organized as follows. In §
2
, we give some necessary preliminaries. In §
3
, for a given weight
\lambda
, we explain our method of computing
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
, where we write
\lambda=w_{\lambda}\mu
such that
w_{\lambda}
has the minimal length in the Weyl group
W_{[\lambda]}
and
\mu
is antidominant. In §
5
–§
6
, we give the GK dimensions of all the simple highest weight modules for exceptional Lie algebras. In §
7
–§
8
, we show how to determine the annihilator varieties of the highest weight modules for all exceptional Lie algebras, by using our results in §
3
coupled with the Sommers duality. Throughout, we use Bourbaki’s notation and coordinates for root systems and the relevant data, as given in
[
Bou02
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some basic results on Lusztig’s
\mathbf{a}
-functions, Gelfand–Kirillov dimensions and associated varieties. See
[
Lus84
,
Vog78
,
Vog91
]
for more details.

Now let
\mathfrak{g}
be a simple complex Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{h}
be a Cartan subalgebra.
Let
\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi
be the set of positive roots determined by a Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. So we have a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-}
with
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{n}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
. Denote by
\Delta
the set of simple roots in
\Phi^{+}
.

2.1.
Hecke algebra and
\mathbf{a}
-functions

Recall that the Weyl group
W
of
\mathfrak{g}
is a Coxeter group generated by
S:=\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Delta\}
. Let
\ell(-)
be the length function on
W
with respect to
S
. Then we have a Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
over
\mathcal{A}:=\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
, which is generated by
T_{w}
,
w\in W
with relations

T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}=T_{ww^{\prime}}\text{ if }\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime}),

\text{and }(T_{s}+q^{-1})(T_{s}-q)=0\text{ for any }s\in S.

The Kazhdan–Lusztig basis
C_{w},w\in W
of
\mathcal{H}
are characterized as the unique elements
C_{w}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\overline{C_{w}}=C_{w},\qquad C_{w}\equiv T_{w}\mod{\mathcal{H}_{<0}}

where
\bar{\,}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is the bar involution such that
\bar{q}=q^{-1}
,
\overline{T_{w}}=T_{w^{-1}}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{<0}=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\mathcal{A}_{<0}T_{w}
,
\mathcal{A}_{<0}=q^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[q^{-1}]
.

If
C_{y}
occurs in the expansion of
hC_{w}
(resp.
C_{w}h
) with respect to the KL-basis for some
h\in\mathcal{H}
, then we write
y\leftarrow_{L}w
(resp.
y\leftarrow_{R}w
). Extend
\leftarrow_{L}
(resp.
\leftarrow_{R}
) to a preorder
\leqslant_{L}
(resp.
\leqslant_{R}
) on
W
. For
x,w\in W
, write
x\leqslant_{LR}w
if there exist
x=w_{1},\dots,w_{n}=w
such that for every
1\leqslant i<n
we have either
w_{i}\leqslant_{L}w_{i+1}
or
w_{i}\leqslant_{R}w_{i+1}
. Let
\sim_{L}
,
\sim_{R}
,
\sim_{LR}
be the equivalence relations associated with
\leqslant_{L}
,
\leqslant_{R}
,
\leqslant_{LR}
(for example,
x\sim_{L}w
if and only if
x\leqslant_{L}w
and
w\leqslant_{L}x
). The equivalence classes on
W
for
\sim_{L}
,
\sim_{R}
,
\sim_{LR}
are called
left cells
,
right cells
and
two-sided cells
respectively.

We have
C_{x}C_{y}=\sum_{z\in W}h_{x,y,z}C_{z}
with
h_{x,y,x}\in\mathcal{A}
. Then Lusztig’s
\mathbf{a}
-function

\mathbf{a}:W\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is defined by

\mathbf{a}(z)=\max\{\deg h_{x,y,z}\mid x,y\in W\}\text{ for }z\in W.

The following lemma is an easy consequence of Lusztig’s results
[
Lus03
, §15]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
x,w\in W
. Then

(1)

\mathbf{a}(w)=\mathbf{a}(w^{-1})
.

(2)

If
x\leqslant_{LR}w
, then
\mathbf{a}(x)\geqslant\mathbf{a}(w)
. Hence
\mathbf{a}(x)=\mathbf{a}(w)
whenever
x\sim_{LR}w
.

(3)

If
w_{I}
is the longest element of the parabolic subgroup of
W
generated by a subset
I\subseteq S
, the
\mathbf{a}(w_{I})
is equal to the length
\ell(w_{I})
of
w_{I}
.

(4)

If
W
is a direct product of Coxeter subgroups
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
, then

\mathbf{a}(w)=\mathbf{a}(w_{1})+\mathbf{a}(w_{2})

for
w=(w_{1},w_{2})\in W_{1}\times W_{2}=W
.

Consider

\mathcal{C}:=\{w\in W\backslash\{1\}:\ w\text{ has a unique reduced expression}\}.

Denote
\mathcal{C}w_{0}:=\{ww_{0}\mid w\in\mathcal{C}\}
, where
w_{0}
is the longest element of
W
. From
[
Lus83
, Prop. 3.8]
, we know that
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}w_{0}
are two-sided cells of
W
.

Recall that Lusztig singled out a subclass of representations of Weyl groups called
special
in
[
Lus84
]
. Roughly speaking, for classical types, special representations are characterized by special symbols (equivalently special partitions). For exceptional types, special representations are listed as the first characters in each family of symbols
[
Lus84
, Chap. 4]
.

Special orbits are then by definitions those corresponding to the special representations via the Springer correspondence, which we briefly recall now. Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{C}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Denote by
\mathcal{N}(G)
the set of nilpotent orbits in
\mathfrak{g}
. For
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{N}(G)
, we write
A_{\mathcal{O}}:=G_{\rm ad}^{x}/(G_{\rm ad}^{x})^{o}
for the connected component group of the centralizer subgroup of any
x\in\mathcal{O}
in the adjoint group
G_{\rm ad}
of
G
. Consider

\mathcal{N}^{\rm en}(G):=\{(\mathcal{O},\eta):\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{N}(G),\eta\in{\rm Irr}(A_{\mathcal{O}})\}.

The Springer correspondence gives an injective map

(2.1)

{\rm Spr}_{G}:{\rm Irr}(W(G))\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\rm en}(G)

denoted by
{\rm Spr}_{G}(\sigma)=(\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G}(\sigma),\eta_{\sigma})
. In particular
{\rm Spr}_{G}(\mathbf{1})=(\mathcal{O}_{\rm reg},\mathbf{1})
and
{\rm Spr}_{G}(\varepsilon_{W})=(0,\mathbf{1})
.
For every orbit
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{N}(G)
, the pair
(\mathcal{O},\mathbf{1})
always lies in the image of
{\rm Spr}_{G}
, and thus we have a well-defined map

{\rm Spr}_{G,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}:\mathcal{N}(G)\longrightarrow{\rm Irr}(W(G))

given by
{\rm Spr}_{G,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}):={\rm Spr}_{G}^{-1}(\mathcal{O},\mathbf{1})
. If
\sigma\in{\rm Irr}(W(G))
is a special representation, then one has
{\rm Spr}_{G}(\sigma)=(\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G}(\sigma),\mathbf{1})
, and we call
\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G}(\sigma)
a special representation, as alluded to above.

In fact, it can be seen that
{\rm Spr}_{G}
actually only depends on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and thus we may write all the above using
\mathfrak{g}
as

{\rm Spr}_{\mathfrak{g}}:={\rm Spr}_{G},\quad\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G},\quad{\rm Spr}_{\mathfrak{g},\mathbf{1}}^{-1}:={\rm Spr}_{G,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}.

On the other hand, in
[
BXX23
]
or
[
BMW25
]
, from a classical Weyl group element
w\in W
, we can get a special partition
{\bf p}
and a special symbol
\Lambda_{\bf p}
(representing the irreducible special representation
{\rm Spr}_{\mathfrak{g},\mathbf{1}}^{-1}({\bf p})
). Then we can compute the corresponding
\mathbf{a}(w)
from this symbol and write
\mathbf{a}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})=\mathbf{a}(w)
since the
\mathbf{a}
-function is constant on a two-sided cell by Lemma
2.1
. The following lemma lists all the possible special partitions for a given
\mathbf{a}
value. It will be used later.

Lemma 2.2
.

We denote
\mathbf{a}({\bf p}):=\mathbf{a}(\Lambda_{{\bf p}})
.

(1)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{1}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{2}].\end{cases}

(2)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{2}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2,1],\\
3&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{3}].\end{cases}

(3)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{3}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,1],\\
2&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{2}],\\
3&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2,1^{2}],\\
6&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{4}].\end{cases}

(4)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{4}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[5],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,1],\\
2&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,2],\\
3&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,1^{2}],\\
4&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{2},1],\\
6&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2,1^{3}],\\
10&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{5}].\\
\end{cases}

(5)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{5}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[6],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[5,1],\\
2&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,2],\\
3&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,1^{2}]\emph{~or~}[3^{2}],\\
4&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,2,1],\\
6&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,1^{3}]\emph{~or~}[2^{3}],\\
7&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{2},1^{2}],\\
10&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2,1^{4}],\\
15&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{5}].\\
\end{cases}

(6)

The
\mathbf{a}
-function of the Weyl group of type
A_{7}
takes the following values:

\mathbf{a}({\bf p})=\begin{cases}0&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[8],\\
1&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[7,1],\\
2&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[6,2],\\
3&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[6,1^{2}]\emph{~or~}[5,3],\\
4&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[5,2,1]\emph{~or~}[4^{2}],\\
5&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,3,1],\\
6&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[5,1^{3}]\emph{~or~}[4,2^{2}],\\
7&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,2,1^{2}]\emph{~or~}[3^{2},2],\\
8&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3^{2},1^{2}],\\
\end{cases}\emph{~and~}\mathbf{a}(\bf p)=\begin{cases}9&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,2^{2},1],\\
10&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[4,1^{4}],\\
11&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,2,1^{3}],\\
12&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{4}],\\
13&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{3},1^{2}],\\
15&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[3,1^{5}],\\
16&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2^{2},1^{4}],\\
21&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[2,1^{6}],\\
28&\emph{if}{\bf p}=[1^{8}].\\
\end{cases}

-function of the Weyl group of type
