We also have the following result for nonintegral highest weight modules of
E_{8}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
L(\lambda)
be a simple nonintegral highest weight module of
E_{8}
. Assume that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal.
Then
one of the following holds:

(1)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{8}
. Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

64,77,78,86,89,90,91,92,96,97,98,99,100,[102,120].

(2)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{8}
. Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

84,92,98,99,102,104,105,[107,120].

(3)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq E_{7}\times A_{1}
.
Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

56,57,73,74,82,83,84,89,90,94,95,97,98,99,100,[103,120].

(4)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{7}\times A_{1}
.
Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

91,92,98,99,[103,120].

(5)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{5}\times A_{2}\times A_{1}
and

\Delta_{[\lambda]}=\{\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}\}.

Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

[101,120].

(6)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{4}\times A_{4}
and

\Delta_{[\lambda]}=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1},\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{4},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8}\}.

Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

100,104,[106,120].

(7)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{5}\times A_{3}
. Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

94,[97,102],[104,120].

(8)

\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq E_{6}\times A_{2}
. Then

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
can achieve the following values:

81,83,84,92,94,95,97,99,100,102,[104,120].

Proof.

The argument is similar to that in the proof of Proposition
6.2
.

∎

Example 6.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
and let
L(\lambda)
be the highest weight module of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\lambda=(\frac{1}{4},\frac{5}{4},\frac{9}{4},\frac{13}{4},\frac{9}{4},\frac{1}{4},\frac{5}{4},\frac{9}{4}).
It is easy to verify that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
is a subsystem with simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\gamma\}
where
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{5}-\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{8})
. Therefore
\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{8}
. We define the isomorphism
\phi:\Phi_{[\lambda]}\rightarrow D_{8}
such that
\phi(\alpha_{1})=\beta_{1},\phi(\alpha_{3})=\beta_{2},\phi(\alpha_{4})=\beta_{3},\phi(\alpha_{5})=\beta_{4},\phi(\alpha_{6})=\beta_{5},

\phi(\alpha_{7})=\beta_{6},\phi(\alpha_{8})=\beta_{7},\phi(\gamma)=\beta_{8}
.
Then by the algorithm in §
3.2
,

\displaystyle\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-4\phi(\omega_{1})+\phi(\omega_{2})+\phi(\omega_{3})+\phi(\omega_{4})-\phi(\omega_{5})-2\phi(\omega_{6})+\phi(\omega_{7})+5\phi(\omega_{8})

\displaystyle=(-1,3,2,1,0,1,3,2)

is an integral weight of type
D_{8}
.
So
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda^{\prime}})=17
by the RS algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
and Proposition
2.10
.
By Proposition
2.8
, we have
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=|\Phi^{+}|-\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=120-17=103
.

Example 6.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
and
L(\lambda)
be the highest weight module of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\lambda=(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{3}{2},-3,-2,-1,-4,-5,-19)
. It is easy to verify that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
is a subsystem with simple roots
\{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1},\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\alpha_{2}\}
. Therefore
\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq E_{7}\times A_{1}
. Suppose the simple root system of
E_{7}\times A_{1}
is
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}\times\{\gamma_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}\}
.
We define the isomorphism
\phi:\Phi_{[\lambda]}\rightarrow E_{7}\times A_{1}
such that
\phi(\alpha_{8})=\alpha_{1},\phi(\alpha_{7})=\alpha_{3},\phi(\alpha_{6})=\alpha_{4},\phi(\alpha_{5})=\alpha_{2},\phi(\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4})=\alpha_{5},\phi(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{6},\phi(\alpha_{3})=\alpha_{7},\phi(\alpha_{2})=\gamma_{1}
.

Denote
{\lambda}^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})
.
Thus by the algorithm in §
3.2
,

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}|_{{E_{7}}}=(2,-1,0,-5,-6,-8,12,-12)

is an integral weight of type
E_{7}
, and

\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
is an integral weight of type
A_{1}
with
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A_{1}}}})=0
by using the RS algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
and Proposition
2.10
.

Denote
\gamma=\phi({\lambda})|_{E_{7}}=(2,-1,0,-5,-6,-8,12,-12)
.
We can write
\gamma=-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-3\omega_{3}+\omega_{4}-5\omega_{5}-\omega_{6}-2\omega_{7}:=[-1,1,-3,1,-5,-1,-2]
. By using Lemma
3.2
, we find that

s_{4}s_{2}s_{4}\gamma=[-1,-1,-1,-1,-3,-1,-2],

which is antidominant. Thus we have
w_{\gamma}=s_{4}s_{2}s_{4}
.
Note that
w_{\gamma}=[3,1,3]
in PyCox. Then by using PyCox, we find that
\mathbf{a}(w_{\gamma})=3
.
So
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda^{\prime}})=3+0=3
.
By Proposition
2.8
, we have
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=|\Phi^{+}|-\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=120-3=117
.

7.
Annihilator varieties of highest weight modules

Recall that one can compute the annihilator variety
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
by applying the result of Joseph
[
Jos85
]
. The goal of this section is to show that one method of computation is provided by the Sommers duality as defined in
[
Som01
]
. Note that in many cases, the dimension of a nilpotent orbit already determines uniquely the orbit itself, and thus such a computation can be avoided in those cases.

Joseph’s result (see Proposition
2.13
) asserts that

(7.1)

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G}\circ j_{W_{[\lambda]}}^{W(G)}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}).

Hence, in principle, one can use (
7.1
) to compute the annihilator variety of
L(\lambda)
. However, explicating the
j
-induction in each case is non-trivial. Instead, we show that the right hand side of (
7.1
) is intimately related to the Sommers duality studied in
[
Som01
]
, which entails a more direct formula in computing
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
.

We use notation as in §
2.1
. Let
G
be an algebraic group over
\mathbb{C}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Recall that a pseudo-Levi subgroup
M\subseteq G
is the connected centralizer subgroup of a semisimple element of
G
. Such a group is essentially characterized by the fact that the Dynkin diagram of its root system is a subset of the extended Dynkin diagram of the root system of
G
. We consider pairs of the form
(M,\mathcal{O}_{M})
, where
M
is a pseudo-Levi subgroup and
\mathcal{O}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}(M)
is a distinguished orbit. Let
G^{\vee}
be the Langlands dual group of
G
. Then Sommers defined a natural map

d_{\rm Som}^{G}:\{(M,\mathcal{O}_{M})\}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(G^{\vee})

that extends the Barbasch–Vogan dual map
d_{\rm BV}:\mathcal{N}(G)\to\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G^{\vee})
, where
\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G)\subseteq\mathcal{N}(G)
denotes the subset of special nilpotent orbits of
G
. This we explain in more detail now.

First, one has the Lusztig–Spaltenstein map
d_{\rm LS}^{G}:\mathcal{N}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G)
. Moreover, one has a natural isomorphism

\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}:W(G)\longrightarrow W(G^{\vee})

of the two Weyl groups, which induces a bijection (using the same notation)

\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}:{\rm Irr}(W(G))\longrightarrow{\rm Irr}(W(G^{\vee})).

It furthermore induces a bijection (by abuse of notation again)

\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}:\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G^{\vee}).

One has
d_{\rm BV}:=\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}\circ d_{\rm LS}^{G}
. All these maps mentioned above fit into the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}^{\rm spe}(G^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(G^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{(M,\mathcal{O}_{M})\}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\rm LS}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\rm BV}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\rm Som}^{G}}

Here the bottom injection is provided by the Bala–Carter classification of nilpotent orbits, asserting that every
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{N}(G)
corresponds uniquely to a
G
-conjugacy class of pair
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
, where
L\subseteq G
is a Levi subgroup and
\mathcal{O}_{L}
a distinguished orbit for
L
. In this case, we write

{\rm sat}_{L}^{G}(\mathcal{O}_{L}):=G\cdot\mathcal{O}_{L}\in\mathcal{N}(G)

for the nilpotent orbit thus obtained. We remark also that Achar actually gave a further extension of
d_{\rm Som}^{G}
in
[
Ach03
]
.

Let
M\subseteq G
be a pseudo-Levi subgroup and
\mathcal{O}_{M}
a distinguished orbit for
M
. Then it was shown by Sommers
[
Som01
, §6]
that

(7.2)

d_{\rm Som}^{G}(M,\mathcal{O}_{M})=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M)}^{W(G)}\circ{\rm Spr}_{M,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}\circ d_{\rm LS}^{M}(\mathcal{O}_{M}).

Note that the dual group
M^{\vee}
of a pseudo-Levi
M
of
G
in general may not be a pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G^{\vee}
. However, one has a natural isomorphism
W(M)\simeq W(M^{\vee})
. Also, for every
\sigma\in{\rm Irr}(W(M))
, one
\sigma^{\vee}:=\tau_{M,M^{\vee}}(\sigma)\in{\rm Irr}(W(M^{\vee}))
satisfying

(7.3)

j_{W(M^{\vee})}^{W(G^{\vee})}(\sigma^{\vee})=\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M)}^{W(G)}(\sigma).

Proposition 7.1
.

Keep notations as above. Assume
\sigma\in{\rm Irr}(W(M))
is a special representation of
W(M)
. Furthermore, let
L\subseteq M
be a Levi subgroup of
M
and
\mathcal{O}_{L}
be a distinguished orbit of
L
such that
d_{\rm LS}^{M}\circ\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{M}(\sigma)={\rm sat}_{L}^{M}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
.
Then

\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M^{\vee})}^{W(G^{\vee})}(\sigma^{\vee})=d_{\rm Som}^{G}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L}).

Proof.

Note that
L
is a Levi subgroup of
M
and
M
is a pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G
. Thus,
L
is a pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G
and the right hand side of the above equality is well-defined.

Now we have from (
7.3
) that

\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M^{\vee})}^{W(G^{\vee})}(\sigma^{\vee})=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M)}^{W(G)}(\sigma).

Since
\sigma
is a special representation of
W(M)
and
d_{\rm LS}^{M}
is a bijection when restricted to the special orbits of
M
, it follows that

\sigma={\rm Spr}_{M,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}\circ d_{\rm LS}^{M}\circ{\rm sat}_{L}^{M}(\mathcal{O}_{L}).

Consider the induction of nilpotent orbits
{\rm ind}_{L}^{M}:\mathcal{N}(L)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(M)
. One has

d_{\rm LS}^{M}\circ{\rm sat}_{L}^{M}(\mathcal{O}_{L})={\rm ind}_{L}^{M}\circ d_{\rm LS}^{L}(\mathcal{O}_{L}),

see
[
CM93
, Theorem 8.3.1]
. On the other hand, the Springer correspondence intertwins the maps
j_{W(L)}^{W(M)}
and
{\rm ind}_{L}^{M}
(see
[
LS79
]
), i.e.,

{\rm Spr}_{M,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}\circ{\rm ind}_{L}^{M}=j_{W(L)}^{W(M)}\circ{\rm Spr}_{L,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}.

Combining the above four equalities and using
j_{W(M)}^{W(G)}\circ j_{W(L)}^{W(M)}=j_{W(L)}^{W(G)}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(M^{\vee})}^{W(G^{\vee})}(\sigma^{\vee})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{G^{\vee}}\circ\tau_{G,G^{\vee}}\circ j_{W(L)}^{W(G)}\circ{\rm Spr}_{L,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}\circ d_{\rm LS}^{L}(\mathcal{O}_{L})

\displaystyle=d_{\rm Som}^{G}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L}),

where the last equality follows from (
7.3
). This completes the proof.
∎

Now Proposition
7.1
coupled with (
7.1
) enable us to compute
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
more efficiently, by utilizing the tabulated data for the Sommers duality given in
[
Som01
,
Ach03
]
. More precisely, we apply Proposition
7.1
with the role of
G
and
G^{\vee}
switched, while
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}
substitutes the input in the left hand side of the equality in Proposition
7.1
. This will enable us to completely determine the column of
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
in the tables given as in the Appendix. In the remaining of this section, we give an elaboration on this.

In
[
BXX23
]
, by using the RS algorithm for
x\in\mathrm{Seq}_{n}(\Gamma)
, we can get a Young tableau
P(x)
and a partition
{\bf p}(x)=\mathrm{sh}(P(x))=p(x)=[p_{1},p_{2},...,p_{N}]
, where
p_{i}
is the number of boxes in the
i
-th
row of
P(x)
.
Recall that in
[
BMW25
]
, we can get a special partition
H(p(x^{-}))
from the given Young tableau
P(x^{-})
or the partition
{\bf p}(x^{-})=p(x^{-})
by using the H-algorithm defined in
[
BMW25
]
. We denote it by
p_{X}(x^{-})^{s}
when it is a special partition of type
X
(for
X=B,C
or
D
). Roughly speaking,
H(p(x^{-}))=p_{X}(x^{-})^{s}
is the unique special partition which has the same odd (resp. even) boxes for type
B

and
C
(resp. type
D
) with the partition
p(x^{-})
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a Cartan subalgebra.
Let
\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi
be the set of positive roots determined by a Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. We have a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-}
with
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{n}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
. Denote by
\Delta
the set of simple roots in
\Phi^{+}
.
By the results in §
3.2
, we may write
\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{k}
for any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and

\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}.

Thus
{\lambda}^{\prime}|_{\Phi_{i}}
is an integral weight of type
\Phi_{i}
. When some
\Phi_{i}
is of type
A
, we can get a special partition
{\bf p}_{i}:=p(\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}})
of type
A
which comes from the Young tableau
P(\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}})
.
When some
\Phi_{i}
is of classical type
X_{i}
(
X_{i}=B,C
or
D
), by using the H-algorithm in
[
BMW25
]
, we can get two special partitions
{\bf p}_{i}:=p_{X_{i}}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}})^{-})^{s}
of type
X_{i}
and
{\bf p}^{\vee}_{i}:=p_{X_{i}^{\vee}}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}})^{-})^{s}
of type
X_{i}^{\vee}
, both of which come from the Young tableau
P((\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}})^{-})
. When some
\Phi_{i}
is of exceptional type, we can use Lemma
3.2
and PyCox to get the corresponding character
\chi_{i}
of
w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}}
and the corresponding special nilpotent orbit (Bala–Carter label)
\mathcal{O}_{\chi_{i}}
.

For classical types, we identify a special partition
{\bf p}
(corresponding to a special nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}_{w}
) and its corresponding special representation
\pi_{w}=\pi_{\bf p}
of the Weyl group by the Springer correspondence. We write
{\bf p}^{t}
for the transpose of a partition
{\bf p}
. Also, for a partition
{\bf p}
of classical
X
-type, one has
d_{\rm LS}({\bf p})={\bf p}^{t}_{X}
.

From now on, we identify a root subsystem of
\Phi
with the corresponding analytic subgroup of the given Lie group
G
. Thus
\Phi
is identified with
G
. We may write
L\subseteq\Phi
or
\mathcal{O}_{L}\subseteq\Phi
to mean that
L
is a Levi subgroup of
G
or
\mathcal{O}_{L}
is the distinguished orbit for
L
.

Corollary 7.2
.

Keep notations as above. Suppose the root system of
\mathfrak{g}
is
\Phi
. Let
L(\lambda)
be a highest weight module of
\mathfrak{g}
. Denote

H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}\times...\times\Phi_{k}
. Write
\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}
. We have the following possibilities:

(1)

Suppose
\Phi_{i}
is of classical type
X_{i}
and
{\bf p}_{i}
is not a very even partition of type
D
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. Then
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}{\bf p}_{i}\in{\rm Irr}(W(H))
,
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}{\bf p}^{\vee}_{i}\in{\rm Irr}(W(H^{\vee}))
, and

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}))=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}({\bf p}^{\vee}_{i})^{t}_{X_{i}}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
.
Also we have

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{G^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}).

(2)

Suppose
\Phi_{i}
is of classical type
X_{i}
and simply-laced for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
and one unique
{\bf p}_{i_{0}}
is a very even partition of type
D
. Then the numeral of
\mathcal{O}_{L}
is the same with the numeral of
\pi_{{\bf p}_{i_{0}}}
.

(3)

Suppose there is a unique
\Phi_{i_{0}}
of exceptional type
E
and all other
\Phi_{i}
is of classical type
X_{i}
and simply-laced.
Suppose that
\chi_{i_{0}}
is the corresponding character for
w_{\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i_{0}}}}}
with Bala–Carter label
L_{i_{0}}
. Then
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}\simeq\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=\chi_{i_{0}}\times\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\neq i_{0}\leqslant k}{\bf p}_{i}
and
\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee})=L_{i_{0}}\times\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\neq i_{0}\leqslant k}{\bf p}_{i}
.
Also we have

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})

for a distinguished orbit
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
of
L^{\vee}
,
and thus

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{G^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}).

Recall that we use
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}
to denote the simple system of the root system
\Phi
for a simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\tilde{\Delta}=\Delta\cup\{\alpha_{0}\}
, where
-\alpha_{0}=\beta
is the highest root of
\Phi
. We write
\theta=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
and set
c_{0}=1
. For any proper subset
J\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
, let
d_{J}
be the greatest common divisor of those
c_{i}
for which
\alpha_{i}\in\tilde{\Delta}-J
.

The following result without proof will be used in the determination of the Bala–Carter label for a given
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
. Some details can be found in
[
Car85
,
Som98
]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
{\bf p}
be a partition corresponding to a nilpotent orbit of classical type
(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})
. Then some correspondences are given in Table
2
.

For type
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, it is possible that there are two in-equivalence classes
L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime}
(both of type
A_{r}
) associated with
J^{\prime},J^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
respectively. Since such
L
is of type
A_{r}
, a distinguished
\mathcal{O}_{L}
is the regular orbit of
L
. Here
L^{\prime}
in
the Bala–Carter label indicates that
J^{\prime}
is conjugate to a subset of
A_{k}\subseteq E_{k}
(
k=7,8
); otherwise we have
L^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover, for
E_{8}
we have that
d_{J}=1
if and only if the decoration is given by
L^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.4
.

In Table
1
we elaborate on the different conventions and notation used in literature regarding very even orbits and their associated Weyl group representations. The notation is either self-explanatory, or a precise reference is given.

Table 1.
Conventions for very even orbits and representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2n,\mathbb{C})

Our notation

{\bf p}^{I}

{\bf p}^{II}

[
Lus84
, p. 89]

\pi^{\prime}

\pi^{\prime\prime}

[
GP00
, p. 170]

([\bf u],+)

([\bf u],-)

PyCox

([\bf u],-)

([\bf u],+)

[
CM93
, p. 83]

\mathcal{O}^{I}
if
n\equiv 0~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

\mathcal{O}^{II}
if
n\equiv 0~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

\mathcal{O}^{II}
if
n\equiv 2~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

\mathcal{O}^{I}
if
n\equiv 2~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

[
Alv05
]

([\bf u],[\bf u])^{+}
if
n\equiv 0~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

([\bf u],[\bf u])^{-}
if
n\equiv 0~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

([\bf u],[\bf u])^{-}
if
n\equiv 2~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

([\bf u],[\bf u])^{+}
if
n\equiv 2~(\mathrm{mod}~4)

The annihilator variety
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))
can be determined by
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
if there is only one nilpotent orbit with dimension equal to
2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
. Thus we only need to consider the cases when there is more than one nilpotent orbit with dimension equal to
2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
.
For
L(\lambda)
with
\lambda
integral, the results are given
in Propositions
5.1
,
4.1
,
6.1
,
6.4
and
6.7
.

7.1.
Type
G_{2}

If
L(\lambda)
is a simple highest weight module of
G_{2}
, the annihilator variety
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))
can be determined uniquely by
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
since all the nilpotent orbits have different dimensions, see
[
CM93
, §8.4]
. Thus the annihilator variety
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))
for type
G_{2}
can be determined by our algorithm of GK dimensions in §
3
.

7.2.
Type
F_{4}

We only need to consider the nonintegral cases and the results are as follows.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
L(\lambda)
be a simple nonintegral highest weight module of
F_{4}
such that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal. Write
\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}
.
Then the following holds:

(1)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq C_{4}
.
We only need to consider the following three cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15,18,21.

In the above three cases, we have
H^{\vee}=B_{4}
,

\pi_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}=p_{C}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}
and
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}^{\vee}_{1}=p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}=({\bf p}_{1}+[1,0,\cdots,0])_{B}
. Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}))=({\bf p}^{\vee}_{1})^{t}_{B_{4}}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
is a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
11
.

(2)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{2}\times\tilde{A}_{2}
. We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=18,21.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}=\tilde{A}_{2}\times A_{2}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{2}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{\tilde{A}_{2}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}^{t}_{1}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
12
.

(3)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{1}\times\tilde{A}_{3}
. We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=18,21.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}=\tilde{A}_{1}\times A_{3}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{\tilde{A}_{3}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}^{t}_{1}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
13
.

(4)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq B_{3}\times\tilde{A}_{1}
. We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15,21.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}={C}_{3}\times A_{1}
,

\pi_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{B_{3}})^{-})^{s}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
and
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}^{\vee}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{C}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{B_{3}})^{-})^{s}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{\tilde{A}_{1}})
. Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}))=({\bf p}^{\vee}_{1})^{t}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
14
.

Proof.

We give some details for (1), (4) and omit the argument for (2) and (3) since the latter is similar and easier.

For (1), we have

H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq C_{4}
with
H^{\vee}=B_{4}
being a pseudo-Levi subgroup of
F_{4}
. From
\lambda^{\prime}
, by using the H-algorithm in
[
BMW25
]
, we can get two special partitions
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}=p_{C}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}
of type
C_{4}
and
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}^{\vee}_{1}=p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}
of type
B_{4}
, which both come from the Young tableau
P((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})
. Then
p_{C}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}
will give us the special bipartition
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=({\bf d},{\bf f})\in{\rm Irr}(W(H))
.
Consider the representation
\sigma^{\vee}=\tau_{H,H^{\vee}}(\sigma)=({\bf d},{\bf f})\in{\rm Irr}(W(H^{\vee}))
. A simple computation gives that

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}\circ\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{H^{\vee}}(\sigma^{\vee})=(p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime}))^{-})^{s})^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq F_{4}^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
is a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
.
It follows from Proposition
7.1
that

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}).

When
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15,18
or
21
, there are more than one nilpotent orbits with
dimension equal to
2\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)
.
For example, when
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15
, we should have
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=9
. By Lemma
2.2
, there is only one special partition of type
C_{4}
whose
\mathbf{a}
value is
9
:
{\bf p}=[2,2,1^{4}]
.
The corresponding special partition of type
B_{4}
is
{\bf p}^{\vee}=({\bf p}+[1,0,\cdots,0])_{B}=[3,1^{6}]
. Thus
({\bf p}^{\vee})^{t}=[7,1,1]={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})
, where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=B_{3}
.
Thus from Proposition
7.1
and the LS duality in
[
Car85
, p. 440]
or Table
8
, we have
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))=\overline{\mathcal{O}}(A_{2})
since
d_{\rm Som}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=d_{\rm LS}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=A_{2}
when
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=B_{3}
.

The argument for
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=18
and
21
is similar.

For (4) one has
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq B_{3}\times\tilde{A}_{1}
and
H^{\vee}={C}_{3}\times A_{1}
. By Lemma
2.2
, we only need to consider that
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15
or
21
since
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})
can not take the value
6
. For example, when
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=21
, we should have
\mathbf{a}(w_{\lambda})=3
. By Lemma
2.2
, there are two special partitions of type
B_{3}\times{A}_{1}
whose
\mathbf{a}
value is
3
:
{\bf p}=[3,3,1]\times[1,1]
and
{\bf q}=[3,2,2]\times[2]
.
The corresponding two special partitions of type
C_{3}\times A_{1}
are:
{\bf p}^{\vee}=[3,3]\times[1,1]
and
{\bf q}^{\vee}=[2^{3}]\times[2]
. Thus
({\bf p}^{\vee})^{t}=[2^{3}]\times[2]={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})
where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=2A_{1}+\tilde{A}_{1}
, and
({\bf q}^{\vee})^{t}=[3,3]\times[1,1]={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})
where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=\tilde{A}_{2}
.
Thus from Proposition
7.1
, the LS duality in
[
Car85
, p. 440]
and the Sommers duality in
[
Som01
, Table VII]
or Table
8
, we have
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))=\overline{\mathcal{O}}(B_{3})
for
\pi_{\lambda}={\bf p}
since
d_{\rm Som}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=B_{3}
when
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=2A_{1}+\tilde{A}_{1}
, and
V(\mathrm{Ann}(L(\lambda)))=\overline{\mathcal{O}}(C_{3})
for
\pi_{\lambda}={\bf q}
since
d_{\rm Som}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=d_{\rm LS}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=C_{3}
when
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=\tilde{A}_{2}
.

The argument for
\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=15
is similar.

∎

Example 7.6
.

Recall that in Example
5.5
,

L(\lambda)
is a highest weight module of
F_{4}
with
\lambda=(4,5,\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
is a subsystem with simple roots
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\varepsilon_{2}\}
and
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq C_{4}
. Thus
H^{\vee}\simeq B_{4}
. Suppose the simple root system of
C_{4}
is
\Delta=\{\beta_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2},\beta_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\beta_{3}=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4},\beta_{4}=2\varepsilon_{4}\}
. We define a map
\phi:\Phi_{[\lambda]}\rightarrow C_{4}
such that
\phi(\varepsilon_{2})=\beta_{1},\phi(\alpha_{4})=\beta_{2},\phi(\alpha_{3})=\beta_{3},\phi(\alpha_{2})=\beta_{4}
. Then we have
\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\prime}=(9,-1,2,1)
.

By using the RS algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
and the H-algorithm in
[
BMW25
]
, we have
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=p_{C}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}=[2,2,1^{4}]
and
\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}=p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}=[3,1^{6}]
. A simple computation gives that

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}))=(p_{B}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s})^{t}=[7,1,1]={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=B_{3}
.
It follows from Proposition
7.1
and the LS duality in
[
Car85
, p. 440]
or Table
8
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=d_{\rm LS}^{F_{4}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=\mathcal{O}(A_{2})
when
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=B_{3}
.

7.3.
Type
E_{6}

We only need to consider the nonintegral cases and the results
are as following.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
L(\lambda)
be a simple nonintegral highest weight module of
E_{6}
such that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal. Write
\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}
.
Then
the following holds:

(1)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{5}
. We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=30,32.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}=D_{5}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}=p_{D}((\lambda^{\prime})^{-})^{s}
. Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))=(({\bf p}_{1})^{t})_{D}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
15
.

(2)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{5}\times A_{1}
.
We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=30,32.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{5}\times A_{1}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{5}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A}_{1}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}^{t}_{1}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg})={\rm sat}_{L}^{H}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
16
.

(3)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{2}\times A_{2}\times A_{2}
.
We only need to consider the following two cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=30,32.

In the above two cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{2}\times A_{2}\times A_{2}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}\times{\bf p}_{3}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{2}(1)})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A}_{2}(2)})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A}_{2}(3)})
, where
A_{2}(i)
denotes the
i
-th
A_{2}
component of
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}^{t}_{1}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{3}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
17
.

Proof.

The argument is similar with that in Theorem
7.5
, and thus we omit the details.

∎

7.4.
Type
E_{7}

Recall that in the case of type
D_{2m}
, a nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}
may be associated with a very even partition
{\bf p}
with the numeral being I or II, see Table
1
for our convention. The reader is also referred to
[
BMW25
]
for more details.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
L(\lambda)
be a simple nonintegral highest weight module of
E_{7}
such that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal. Write
\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}
.
Then
the following holds:

(1)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{7}
. We only need to consider the following
12
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=42,47,48,50,51,53,54,55,56,57,59,60.

In the above
12
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{7}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
18
.

(2)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{5}\times A_{2}
. We only need to consider the following
10
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=47,48,50,53,54,55,56,57,59,60.

In the above
10
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{5}\times A_{2}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{5}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{2}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{2}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
19
.

(3)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{3}\times A_{3}\times A_{1}
. We only need to consider the following
9
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=50,51,53,54,55,56,57,59,60.

In the above
9
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{3}\times A_{3}\times A_{1}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}\times{\bf p}_{3}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{3}(1)})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{3}(2)})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{2}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{3}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
20
.

(4)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{6}\times A_{1}
.
We only need to consider the following
12
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=42,47,48,50,51,53,54,55,56,57,59,60.

In the above
12
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=D_{6}\times A_{1}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{D}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{D_{6}})^{-})^{s}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})
. Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))=({\bf p}_{1}^{t})_{D}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
21
and Table
22
. When
{\bf p}_{1}
is a very even partition, the numeral of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
is the same with the numeral of
{\bf p}_{1}
.

(5)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq E_{6}
. We only need to consider the following
8
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=48,50,51,53,56,57,59,60.

In the above
8
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=E_{6}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\chi}_{1}
. Also we have

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})
,
and

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{G^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
23
.

Example 7.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=E_{7}
and
L(\lambda)
be a highest weight module of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\lambda=(1,3,-5,-7,-9,-11,-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. It is easy to verify that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
is a subsystem with simple roots
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}\}
. Therefore
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{6}\times A_{1}
. Suppose the simple root system of
D_{6}\times A_{1}
is
\Delta=\{\beta_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2},\beta_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\beta_{3}=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4},\beta_{4}=\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{5},\beta_{5}=\varepsilon_{5}-\varepsilon_{6},\beta_{6}=\varepsilon_{5}+\varepsilon_{6}\}\times\{\gamma_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2}\}
.
We define a map
\phi:\Phi_{[\lambda]}\rightarrow D_{6}\times A_{1}
such that
\phi(\alpha_{7})=\beta_{1},\phi(\alpha_{6})=\beta_{2},\phi(\alpha_{5})=\beta_{3},\phi(\alpha_{4})=\beta_{4},\phi(\alpha_{3})=\beta_{5},\phi(\alpha_{2})=\beta_{6},\phi(\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7})=\gamma_{1}
.

Thus by the algorithm in §
3.2
,
we have

\lambda^{\prime}|_{D_{6}}=(-11,-9,-7,-5,3,1),

and

\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}}=(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}).

By using the RS algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
and the H-algorithm in
[
BMW25
]
, we have

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{D}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{D_{6}})^{-})^{s}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})=[9,1^{3}]\times[1,1].

Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))=({\bf p}_{1}^{t})_{D}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}=[3,1^{9}]\times[2]={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
by Lemma
7.3
.
It then follows from Proposition
7.1
and the LS duality in
[
Car85
, p. 443]
or Table
9
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{E_{7}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=d_{\rm LS}^{E_{7}^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=\mathcal{O}(E_{6})
when
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.

7.5.
Type
E_{8}

We only need to consider the nonintegral cases and the results
are as follows.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
L(\lambda)
be a simple nonintegral highest weight module of

such that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}

\lambda^{\prime}=\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\phi(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}})|_{{\Phi_{i}}}=\prod\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lambda^{\prime}|_{{\Phi_{i}}}

.

(2)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{7}\times A_{1}
. We only need to consider the following
15
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=92,98,99,102,[104,114],116.

and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{7}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{2}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table

.

(3)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{5}\times A_{2}\times A_{1}
. We only need to consider the following
14
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=101,102,[104,114],116.

In the above
14
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{5}\times A_{2}\times A_{1}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}\times{\bf p}_{3}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{5}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{2}})\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{2}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{3}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
26
and Table
27
.

(4)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{4}\times A_{4}
. We only need to consider the following
12
cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=100,104,[106,114],116.

In the above
12
cases, we have
H^{\vee}=A_{4}\times A_{4}
and

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{4}(1)}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{4}(2)})
. Then

\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}})^{t}={\bf p}_{1}^{t}\times{\bf p}_{2}^{t}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
28
.

(5)

Suppose
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{8}
.
We only need to consider the following

cases:

\operatorname{GKdim}L(\lambda)=92,98,99,100,102,[104,114],116.

In the above

cases, we have
H^{\vee}=D_{8}
and

. Then

d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))=({\bf p}_{1}^{t})_{D}={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
L^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
can be found in Table
29
and Table
30
. When
{\bf p}_{1}
is a very even partition, the numeral of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
is the same with the numeral of
{\bf p}_{1}
.

. Then

and

a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
. The explicit information about

and Table

.

and Table

Remark 7.11
.

In
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
, the partition
{\bf p}=[3,1]
associated with
D_{2}=2A_{1}
corresponds to
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}_{E_{7}}
and
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}_{E_{8}}
respectively. Similarly, the partition
{\bf p}=[5,1]
associated with
D_{3}=A_{3}
corresponds to
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}_{E_{7}}
and
\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}_{E_{8}}
for
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, respectively.

Example 7.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
and
L(\lambda)
be a highest weight module of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\lambda=(1,1,1,1,1,1,\frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{2})
. It is easy to verify that
\Phi_{[\lambda]}
is a subsystem with simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{8}\}
. Therefore
H=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq E_{7}\times A_{1}
. Suppose the simple root system of
E_{7}\times A_{1}
is
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}\times\{\gamma_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2}\}
.
We define a map
\phi:\Phi_{[\lambda]}\rightarrow E_{7}\times A_{1}
such that
\phi(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{1},\phi(\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{2},\phi(\alpha_{3})=\alpha_{3},\phi(\alpha_{4})=\alpha_{4},\phi(\alpha_{5})=\alpha_{5},\phi(\alpha_{6})=\alpha_{6},\phi(\alpha_{7})=\alpha_{7},\phi(\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{8})=\gamma_{1}
.
Thus for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{E_{8}}^{*}
, it will be an integral weight of
{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}
. By the algorithm in §
3.2
,

\lambda^{\prime}|_{{E_{7}}}=(1,1,1,1,1,1,-1,1)

is an integral weight of type
E_{7}
, and

\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A_{1}}}=\left(\frac{3}{2},-\frac{3}{2}\right)

is an integral weight of type
A_{1}
.

Denote
\gamma=\lambda^{\prime}|_{E_{7}}=(1,1,1,1,1,1,-1,1)
.
Then
\gamma=-\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2}:=[-1,2,0,0,0,0,0]
. By using Lemma
3.2
, we can find a
w_{\gamma}
such that

\gamma=w_{\gamma}\mu,

where
\mu=-\omega_{2}-\omega_{7}:=[0,-1,0,0,0,0,-1]
is antidominant and

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}=

\displaystyle[1,3,4,5,6,2,3,4,5,1,3,4,2,3,1,0,2,3,4,5,6,

\displaystyle 1,3,4,5,2,3,4,1,3,2,0,2,3,4,5,1,3,4,2,3,1]

in PyCox. Then by using PyCox, we find that the special character corresponding to
w_{\gamma}
is

105_{b}
and its
\mathbf{a}
value is
6
. From
[
Car85
, p. 430]
, the Bala–Carter label of
\mathcal{O}(105_{b})
is
A_{6}
.

By using the RS algorithm in
[
BX19b
]
, we have

{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})=[1,1].

Now we have

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}=\chi_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}=105_{b}\times p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}})=105_{b}\times[1,1].

Note that from the LS duality in
[
Car85
, p. 443]
, we have
d_{\rm LS}^{E_{7}}(A_{6})=A_{2}+3A_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))

\displaystyle=d_{\rm LS}^{E_{7}}(A_{6})\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}

\displaystyle=d_{\rm LS}^{E_{7}}(A_{6})\times[2]

\displaystyle=(A_{2}+3A_{1})\times[2]

\displaystyle={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=A_{2}+4A_{1}
by Lemma
7.3
.
It follows from Proposition
7.1
and
[
Som01
, Table X]
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=d_{\rm Som}^{E_{8}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}})=\mathcal{O}(D_{7})
.

8.
The general case

For a given highest weight module
L(\lambda)
, the integral dual root system
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
may not be pseudo-maximal. There are two methods of computing
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
. First, since
j
-induction satisfies the property of induction by stages, see
[
Car85
, Proposition 11.2.4]
, from
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}
we can use
j
-induction to get a representation of
W(\Phi_{1})
, where
\Phi_{1}^{\vee}\subseteq\Phi^{\vee}
is pseudo-maximal and
W(\Phi_{1})
contains
W_{[\lambda]}
as parabolic Weyl subgroup. In this case, one can still utilize the tables in Appendix to compute
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
. The second method is to apply Proposition
7.1
directly.

Example 8.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
. We assume
H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq A_{7}
and
\pi_{w_{\lambda}}=[1^{8}]\in\mathrm{Irr}(W(A_{7}))=\mathrm{Irr}(S_{8})
. If we consider
A_{7}
as a root subsystem of
A_{8}
, then from
[
BMW25
]
the
j
-induction gives the representation
\pi_{\bf p}=j_{S_{8}\times S_{1}}^{S_{9}}(\pi_{[1^{8}]}\otimes\pi_{[1]})
of
S_{9}=W(A_{8})
with
{\bf p}=[2,1^{7}]
. It then follows from Table
24
that

\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=\mathcal{O}(D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{2})
.

On the other hand, we see that
d_{\rm LS}^{M^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{M^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm reg})
,
where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}:=(A_{7})^{\prime}
. It then also follows from Proposition
7.1
and
[
Som01
, Table X]
or Table
10
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=\mathcal{O}(D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{2})
.

Let
J=H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
be a proper subset of
\tilde{\Delta}
and
d_{J}
be the greatest common divisor of those
c_{i}
for which
c_{i}\in\tilde{\Delta}-J
. Let
\Phi_{J}
be the root system generated by
J
.
As mentioned in
[
Som98
]
, one needs to execute with care for the following cases of
\Phi_{J}
.

(1)

For type
E_{7}
,
\Phi_{J}
is one the following:

(8.1)

A_{5},A_{5}+A_{1},4A_{1},3A_{1},A_{3}+2A_{1}\text{~or~}A_{3}+A_{1}.

When
J
is conjugate to a subset of
A_{7}\subseteq E_{7}
in the above cases, we give the corresponding root system one prime, otherwise two primes.

(2)

For type
E_{8}
,
\Phi_{J}
is one the following:

(8.2)

A_{7},A_{5}+A_{1},4A_{1},2A_{3}\text{~or~}A_{3}+2A_{1}.

When
J
is conjugate to a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
, we will have
d_{J}=1
in the above cases. Then we give the corresponding root system one prime. When
J
is not conjugate to a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
, we will have
d_{J}=2
and give the corresponding root system two primes.

Example 8.2
.

From Tables
24
–
35
, we see that for most cases the Bala–Carter label of
L
has one prime if the subset
J\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
is a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
, and for most cases it has two primes if
J\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
is not a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
except for the following three cases such that
\mathcal{O}_{L}
corresponds to

[4,1,1]\times[2,1]\times[2],\text{~or~}[2,2,1,1]\times[2,1]\times[2],\text{~or~}[4,4,3,1^{5}].

Note that the first two cases come from Table
26
and Table
27
, and the third one comes from Table
30
.

In the first case, we may choose
J_{1}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
. The corresponding subsystem
\Phi_{J_{1}}
has type
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
or
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
Denote
w_{1}=s_{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}}s_{\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{2}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{1}}\in W(E_{8})
. Then we have
w_{1}(J_{1})=\{\alpha_{1},-\beta,\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}
, which is a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
. Therefore
\Phi_{J_{1}}
corresponds to

\mathcal{O}_{L}=(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
by Lemma
7.3
.

In the second case, we may choose
J_{2}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
. The corresponding subsystem
\Phi_{J_{2}}
has type
(4A_{1})^{\prime}
or
(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
Then we have
w_{1}(J_{2})=\{\alpha_{1},-\beta,\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}
, which is a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
. Therefore
\Phi_{J_{2}}
corresponds to

\mathcal{O}_{L}=(4A_{1})^{\prime}
by Lemma
7.3
.

In the third case, we may choose
J_{3}=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
. The corresponding subsystem
\Phi_{J_{3}}
has type
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
or
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
From
[
Som98
, §2.2]
, we know that
d_{J}
is an invariant of the equivalence class of
J\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
. For
J_{3}
, we have
d_{J_{3}}=\mathrm{gcd}(c_{0},c_{1},c_{4},c_{8})=\mathrm{gcd}(1,2,6,2)=1
. Thus,
J_{3}
conjugates to a subset of
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
by
[
Som98
, §2.2]
.
Therefore,
\Phi_{J_{3}}
corresponds to
\mathcal{O}_{L}=(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
by Lemma
7.3
.

For some cases, it is not easy to determine whether
J
is conjugate to a subset of
A_{7}\subseteq E_{7}
(resp. to a subset
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
). For these cases, we will need to compute the weighted Dynkin diagram of the corresponding nilpotent orbit of the saturation of
d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))
. The algorithm is as follows:

(1)

Find the simple system
\Delta_{[\lambda]}
of
H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}
;

(2)

Suppose
d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))=a_{k}A_{k}+a_{1}A_{1}
for some integer
k>1
and
a_{1},a_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(3)

Find the neutral element of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple associated with the orbit
a_{k}A_{k}+a_{1}A_{1}
from
\Delta_{[\lambda]}
, denoted it by
h:=[b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{l}]=b_{1}\omega_{1}+b_{2}\omega_{2}+\cdots+b_{l}\omega_{l}=c_{1}e_{1}+c_{2}e_{2}+\cdots+c_{8}e_{8}
, where
l=7
for
E_{7}
and
l=8
for
E_{8}
;

(4)

Apply the positive index reduction algorithm in Lemma
3.2
to obtain an antidominant weight and then multiply by
-1
to get a dominant weight
h^{*}
in the Weyl-orbit of
h
;

(5)

Then

(\langle h^{*},\alpha_{i}\rangle)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 7}

or

(\langle h^{*},\alpha_{i}\rangle)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 8}

is the weighted Dynkin diagram for the saturation of the orbit
d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))
in
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
. Then by the tables in
[
Som98
]
or Table
9
and Table
10
, we can determine whether the orbit
a_{k}A_{k}+a_{1}A_{1}
has a decoration of one prime or two primes.

Remark 8.3
.

For each case in (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), we may choose a fixed subset
J\subseteq\tilde{\Delta}
such that
J
is not conjugate to a subset of
A_{7}\subseteq E_{7}
(resp. to a subset
A_{8}\subseteq E_{8}
). Then the corresponding root system
\Phi_{J}
will have two primes.

For type
E_{7}
, we have the following:

(1)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{5})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}
. Then
h=5\alpha_{2}+8\alpha_{4}+9\alpha_{5}+8\alpha_{6}+5\alpha_{7}
.

(2)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{5}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}.
Then
h=\alpha_{1}+5\alpha_{2}+8\alpha_{4}+9\alpha_{5}+8\alpha_{6}+5\alpha_{7}
.

(3)

When
\Phi_{J}=(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}.
Then
h=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{7}
.

(4)

When
\Phi_{J}=(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}.
Then
h=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{7}
.

(5)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7}\}.
Then
h=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+3\alpha_{5}+4\alpha_{6}+3\alpha_{7}
.

(6)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}.
Then
h=3\alpha_{2}+4\alpha_{4}+3\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{7}
.

For type
E_{8}
, we have the following:

(1)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{7})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\alpha_{0}\}.

(2)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{5}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\alpha_{0}\}.

(3)

When
\Phi_{J}=(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{0}\}.

(4)

When
\Phi_{J}=(2A_{3})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{8},\alpha_{0}\}.

(5)

When
\Phi_{J}=(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we may choose
J=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{0}\}.

An explicit form of the neutral element
h
can be found from
[
CM93
, §3.6]
.

Example 8.4
.

Consider
\mathfrak{g}=E_{7}
and

\lambda=(0,1,1,2,3,3,-7,7)/4.

The root system
H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{4}\times A_{1}\times A_{1}
has simple roots

\left\{\alpha:=\frac{1}{2}(-e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}+e_{4}-e_{5}-e_{6}-e_{7}+e_{8}),e_{2}+e_{5},e_{6}-e_{5},e_{3}-e_{2}\right\}

for
D_{4}
and
\{\beta:=\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}-e_{4}-e_{5}-e_{6}-e_{7}+e_{8})\}
for the first
A_{1}
and
\{\gamma:=\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}-e_{4}+e_{5}+e_{6}-e_{7}+e_{8})\}
for the second
A_{1}
.

Thus for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{E_{7}}^{*}
, it will be an integral weight of
{\Phi_{[\lambda]}}
. By the algorithm in §
3.2
,

\lambda^{\prime}|_{{D_{4}}}=(2,1,0,0)

is an integral weight of type
D_{4}
,

\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A_{1}(1)}}=\left(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)

is an integral weight of type
A_{1}
and

\lambda^{\prime}|_{{A_{1}(2)}}=(1,-1)

is an integral weight of type
A_{1}
.

By using the RS algorithm in
[
BXX23
]
and the H-algorithm in
[
BMW25
]
, we have

{\bf p}_{1}=p_{D}((\lambda^{\prime}|_{D_{4}})^{-})^{s}=[3,1^{5}],

{\bf p}_{2}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}(1)})=[1,1],

and

{\bf p}_{3}=p_{A}(\lambda^{\prime}|_{A_{1}(2)})=[1,1].

Thus

\pi^{\vee}_{w_{\lambda}}={\bf p}_{1}\times{\bf p}_{2}\times{\bf p}_{3}=[3,1^{5}]\times[1,1]\times[1,1].

So

\displaystyle d_{\rm LS}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}(\pi_{w_{\lambda}}^{\vee}))

\displaystyle=({\bf p}_{1}^{t})_{D}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{2}\times{\bf p}^{t}_{3}

\displaystyle=[5,1^{3}]\times[2]\times[2]

\displaystyle={\rm sat}_{L^{\vee}}^{H^{\vee}}(\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}).

Moreover, we have

\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}\simeq D_{3}+A_{1}+A_{1}\simeq A_{3}+2A_{1}.

To determine whether it is
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
or
(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we note that the neutral element of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple associated with the orbit
A_{3}+2A_{1}
is of the form

h:=3(e_{3}-e_{2})+4(e_{2}+e_{5})+3(e_{6}-e_{5})+\beta+\gamma=e_{1}+e_{2}+3e_{3}-e_{4}+e_{5}+3e_{6}-e_{7}+e_{8}.

This element is clearly not dominant. We apply the positive index reduction algorithm in Lemma
3.2
to obtain a dominant
h^{*}
in the Weyl-orbit of
h
; also, by
[
CM93
, §3.5 ]
the element
h^{*}
gives rise to a weighted Dynkin diagram decorated by

(\langle h^{*},\alpha_{i}\rangle)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 7}=(0,0,2,0,0,0,0).

This shows that the saturation of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
in
E_{7}
is
D_{4}(a_{1})
by the Table in
[
CM93
, p. 130]
. It then follows (for example, from
[
Som98
, p. 559]
or Table
9
) that
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime}
. It follows from Proposition
7.1
and
[
Som01
, Table IX]
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=D_{6}(a_{2})
.

Example 8.5
.

Consider
\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
and

\lambda=(\omega_{2}+\omega_{7}+2\omega_{8})/4=(1,1,1,1,1,3,7,13)/8.

We obtain that the root system
H:=\Phi_{[\lambda]}\simeq D_{6}\times A_{1}
has simple roots

\left\{\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}-e_{4}-e_{5}-e_{6}-e_{7}+e_{8}),e_{1}+e_{7},-e_{1}+e_{2},-e_{2}+e_{3},-e_{3}+e_{4},-e_{4}+e_{5}\right\}

for
D_{6}
and
\{e_{6}+e_{8}\}
for
A_{1}
. Moreover, by the similar process with Example
8.4
, we have

\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}\simeq(3A_{1})+A_{1}\simeq 4A_{1}.

To determine whether it is
(4A_{1})^{\prime}
or
(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, we note that the neutral element of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple associated with orbit
3A_{1}+A_{1}
is of the form

h:=(e_{5}-e_{4})+(e_{3}-e_{2})+(e_{1}+e_{7})+(e_{6}+e_{8}).

This element is clearly not dominant. We apply the positive index reduction algorithm in Lemma
3.2
to obtain a dominant
h^{*}
in the Weyl-orbit of
h
; also, by
[
CM93
, §3.5 ]

h^{*}
gives rise to a weighted Dynkin diagram decorated by

(\langle h^{*},\alpha_{i}\rangle)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 8}=(0,0,0,0,0,0,0,2).

This shows that the saturation of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
in
E_{8}
is
A_{2}
by the Table in
[
CM93
, p. 130]
. It then follows (for example, from
[
Som98
, p. 559]
or Table
10
) that
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}=(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
. It follows from Proposition
7.1
and
[
Som01
, Table X]
that
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}=E_{7}
.

9.
Appendix

We tabulate all the relevant data when
\Phi_{[\lambda]}^{\vee}
is a pseudo-maximal root subsystem of
\Phi^{\vee}
.
We explain the notation used in the tables:

\bullet

\mathrm{GKd}L(\lambda)
means the Gelfand–Kirillov dimension of
L(\lambda)
;

\bullet

cha
(\mathcal{O}):={\rm Spr}_{G,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})
means the character associated with the nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}
via the Springer correspondence;

\bullet

the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
(as in Tables
8
–
10
) means a distinguished orbit of
L^{\vee}
, where
L^{\vee}\subseteq M^{\vee}
is a Levi group of
M^{\vee}
and
M^{\vee}\subseteq G^{\vee}
is a pseudo-Levi subgroup (thus
L^{\vee}\subseteq G^{\vee}
is a pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G^{\vee}
), as in Proposition
7.1
;

\bullet

\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}^{\rm BC}:=G^{\vee}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
(as in Tables
8
–
10
) means the saturation of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}
, written using the Bala–Carter label;

\bullet

the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{{\rm Ann}(L(\lambda))}
is the annihilator variety of
L(\lambda)
, and is equal to
d_{\rm Som}^{G^{\vee}}(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee}}).

\bullet

\mathcal{O}(\pi):=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{H}(\pi)
in Tables
11
–
14
, where
H^{\vee}\subseteq G^{\vee}
is pseudo-Levi subgroup with root system being a fixed pseudo-maximal root subsystem of
G^{\vee}
, and
\pi
a special representation of
W(H)
; thus
\pi^{\vee}\in{\rm Irr}(W(H^{\vee}))
is special and
\mathcal{O}^{\vee}(\pi)=\mathcal{O}_{\rm Spr}^{H^{\vee}}(\pi^{\vee})
.

For
E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
which are simply-laced, the root system of a Levi and its dual system are canonically identified and of the same type, thus from Table
15
onwards, we make such an identification and omit the column of
\mathcal{O}^{\vee}(\pi)
as in Tables
11
–
14
.
